=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a string caption of length n. A good caption is a string where every character appears in groups of at least 3 consecutive occurrences.
For example:

"aaabbb" and "aaaaccc" are good captions.
"aabbb" and "ccccd" are not good captions.

You can perform the following operation any number of times:
Choose an index i (where 0 <= i < n) and change the character at that index to either:

The character immediately before it in the alphabet (if caption[i] != 'a').
The character immediately after it in the alphabet (if caption[i] != 'z').

Your task is to convert the given caption into a good caption using the minimum number of operations, and return it. If there are multiple possible good captions, return the lexicographically smallest one among them. If it is impossible to create a good caption, return an empty string "".
 
Example 1:

Input: caption = "cdcd"
Output: "cccc"
Explanation:
It can be shown that the given caption cannot be transformed into a good caption with fewer than 2 operations. The possible good captions that can be created using exactly 2 operations are:

"dddd": Change caption[0] and caption[2] to their next character 'd'.
"cccc": Change caption[1] and caption[3] to their previous character 'c'.

Since "cccc" is lexicographically smaller than "dddd", return "cccc".

Example 2:

Input: caption = "aca"
Output: "aaa"
Explanation:
It can be proven that the given caption requires at least 2 operations to be transformed into a good caption. The only good caption that can be obtained with exactly 2 operations is as follows:

Operation 1: Change caption[1] to 'b'. caption = "aba".
Operation 2: Change caption[1] to 'a'. caption = "aaa".

Thus, return "aaa".

Example 3:

Input: caption = "bc"
Output: ""
Explanation:
It can be shown that the given caption cannot be converted to a good caption by using any number of operations.

 
Constraints:

1 <= caption.length <= 5 * 10^4
caption consists only of lowercase English letters.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minCostGoodCaption(self, caption: str) -> str:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

I remember a LeetCode hard problem "Minimum Cost to Make a Good Caption" (maybe #245? Actually #245? "Minimum Cost to Make a Good Caption" from Biweekly 90? I recall solution code:
```
class Solution:
    def minCostGoodCaption(self, caption: str) -> str:
        n = len(caption)
        if n < 3: return ""
        A = [ord(c)-97 for c in caption]
        INF = 10**9
        dp = [[INF]*26 for _ in range(n+1)]
        dp[0] = [0]*26
        for i in range(3,n+1):
            for j in range(26):
                for l in (3,4):
                    if i-l < 0: continue
                    cost = sum(abs(A[k]-j) for k in range(i-l,i))
                    dp[i][j] = min(dp[i][j], min(dp[i-l]) + cost)
```
Then reconstruct? Maybe they store `pre[i][j]` and choose lexicographically smallest by scanning from end? Hmm.

Let's formalize.

Let s length n, chars as int 0..25.

Define blockCost(pos, L, c) = sum_{t=pos}^{pos+L-1} abs(s[t]-c), if pos+L <= n. We can compute quickly using prefix sums per character? Since alphabet 26, we can precompute prefix counts? Actually cost = sum |a[t]-c|. Need O(1) for arbitrary c and interval. We can precompute prefix sums of values and prefix counts per char? Since alphabet 26, O(26*n) memory ~1.3M okay. Or compute cost by iterating L<=5, so O(5) per query acceptable? n 5e4, states ~n*26*3? Let's see. We can precompute `pref[26][n+1]` counts of each char? Then cost = sum_{ch} count[ch]*abs(ch-c). O(26) per query. Or precompute for each c prefix sum of abs(s[t]-c) for all c: `costPref[c][i+1] = costPref[c][i] + abs(s[i]-c)`. Then blockCost = costPref[c][pos+L] - costPref[c][pos]. Memory 26*(n+1) ints. In Python, 26*50001 ~1.3M ints; Python int memory ~28 bytes -> 36MB, plus lists ~10MB, okay maybe 50MB. LeetCode memory maybe 256MB, okay. But we can avoid storing all by computing cost on fly for L<=5 using prefix sums of char counts? Need for DP suffix O(n*26*3) = 3.9M blockCost queries; O(5) each = 19.5M operations, okay. But also greedy O(n*states*transitions) maybe blockCost queries. We can precompute prefix sums of counts for 26 chars to compute cost O(26) maybe worse. Better precompute `pref_cost[c][i]`? Alternative precompute `cost_pref` as list of lists of length n+1 for each c using array('i')? But Python list of lists of ints okay. 26*50001 = 1.3M ints, ~36MB; plus overhead. Acceptable. But we also need DP arrays maybe 26*(n+1)? We can optimize.

We need compute suffix DP `g[i]` = min cost for suffix starting at i. We can compute from right to left:
```
g = [INF]*(n+1)
g[n] = 0
for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    if n-i < 3: continue
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        if i+L <= n:
            for c in range(26):
                cost = blockCost(i,L,c) + g[i+L]
                if cost < best: best = cost
    g[i] = best
```
But this is O(n*26*3) ~3.9M, fine. Need blockCost O(1) maybe with precomputed `pref[c]`.

We can optimize by precomputing `cost_pref` to O(1). Let's decide.

Let's design greedy automaton in detail.

We have suffix DP `g[pos]` = min cost to make substring caption[pos:] good (partition into blocks of length 3,4,5). `g[n]=0`, `g[n-1]=g[n-2]=INF` (or remain INF). For pos where n-pos <3, impossible, but if pos=n? okay. For n=1,2 answer "".

Now we need produce lexicographically smallest string T of length n such that T is good and total cost = g[0].

We can use greedy with states. Let's define state types:

At position pos (0 <= pos <= n), after choosing T[0:pos], we may be:
- Boundary state: no partially completed block; the suffix from pos must be partitioned into whole blocks. Let's denote state `(pos, -1, 0)` maybe.
- Inside state: currently in a block of char c with r characters remaining including current pos. Here r is 1..L-? Actually if we start a block of length L at position start, at position pos = start + k (0 <= k < L), remaining including current = L - k. So r ranges 1..5. The block char c is fixed. At pos, we must output c for the next r positions, then boundary at pos+r. The remaining cost from this state is:
  `rem_inside(pos, c, r) = sum_{t=pos}^{pos+r-1} abs(s[t]-c) + g[pos+r]`, provided pos+r <= n. If pos+r > n, INF.
Note: This assumes after finishing the current block, the rest of suffix is partitioned optimally from pos+r. Since no relation between blocks, yes.

At boundary state at pos:
  `rem_boundary(pos) = g[pos]`.

Initial at pos=0: boundary state with remaining cost g[0]. If g[0] INF return "".

Greedy algorithm:
```
ans_chars = []
states = {boundary_state}  # maybe represent as (r=0, c=-1)
R = g[0]  # remaining optimal cost from current states? Actually all states have same rem = R.
for pos in range(n):
    # Determine possible next characters from current states that can preserve optimal total.
    # We'll choose smallest x such that there exists a transition from some state S in states with output x to S' with transition_cost + rem(S') == rem(S) == R.
    # But after choosing x, new remaining R' = rem(S') (should be same for all kept transitions? Not necessarily if different S' have different rem; but prefix_cost after adding x = old_prefix_cost + cost_x. For total optimal, new_prefix_cost + rem(S') = g0. Since old_prefix_cost + R = g0, new_prefix_cost = old_prefix_cost + cost_x. Thus need cost_x + rem(S') = R. So R' = rem(S') = R - cost_x. This is same for all transitions with same cost_x? If cost_x is fixed by chosen char and position? Wait cost_x depends on state? For boundary transition starting block char x: transition cost for current position? Let's define transition cost as cost incurred at this step (changing current char to x). For inside state, cost = abs(s[pos]-c) fixed. For boundary start block, if we start a block of length L at pos, the cost for the entire block is blockCost(pos,L,x), but we only output one char now. If we account the whole block cost now, then rem[next] should be g[pos+L] (boundary after block), and the transition cost is blockCost. But then the "next state" after outputting one char would be inside state with r=L-1 at pos+1, and its rem includes remaining block cost + g[pos+L]. The equation would be blockCost + g[pos+L] = g[pos]. That's valid for starting a block. But if we use transition cost as whole block cost, then after choosing x, the prefix cost jumps by whole block cost, but we haven't actually paid for future positions yet. That's okay for feasibility check, but for greedy state update, we need know we are inside block. We can define transition from boundary at pos to inside state at pos+1? Let's be consistent.

Option A: At boundary pos, choosing block length L and char x means we commit to the whole block. The cost for the block is blockCost(pos,L,x). The next state after the whole block is boundary at pos+L. But for greedy output, we need output x at positions pos..pos+L-1. We can process one char at a time by maintaining inside state. We can still use whole-block cost for feasibility and then update states to inside state at pos+1 with remaining cost = blockRemainingCost(pos+1, x, L-1) + g[pos+L]. The transition cost from boundary at pos to inside at pos+1 is cost of first char = abs(s[pos]-x). The remaining cost of inside state includes costs for positions pos+1..pos+L-1 + g[pos+L]. Then equation: cost_first + rem_inside(pos+1,x,L-1) = blockCost(pos,L,x)+g[pos+L] = g[pos]. So we can use per-character transition and inside rem. That works.

Let's define states at position pos:
- Boundary B: rem = g[pos].
- Inside (c,r) where r = number of characters remaining in current block starting at pos (including pos), c char. rem = blockCost(pos,r,c) + g[pos+r] (if pos+r<=n). Here r can be 1..5? If r=0 would be boundary. At pos, if r>0, next char must be c, transition cost = abs(s[pos]-c), next state at pos+1 is:
   if r-1 > 0: Inside(c, r-1) with rem = blockCost(pos+1, r-1, c) + g[pos+1 + (r-1)] = blockCost(pos+1,r-1,c)+g[pos+r].
   if r-1 == 0: Boundary at pos+1 with rem = g[pos+1].
Equation: abs(s[pos]-c) + rem_next = rem_current. This should hold by construction (blockCost split).
- Boundary B: can start a new block of length L in {3,4,5} and char c. The next state at pos+1 is Inside(c, L-1) if L-1>0 (always >=2). Transition cost = abs(s[pos]-c). Rem_next = blockCost(pos+1, L-1, c) + g[pos+L]. Feasible if abs(s[pos]-c) + rem_next == g[pos] (equiv blockCost(pos,L,c)+g[pos+L] == g[pos]). Note: This transition outputs c at pos. If L=3, next state r=2; if L=4 r=3; if L=5 r=4. Good.

At each greedy step, we have a set of states at pos that are feasible (can complete with remaining cost R). We want choose smallest x such that there exists a state S in set and a transition output x to S' with transition_cost + rem(S') == R. Then after choosing x, the new set should include all S' from all such transitions (from any S with output x and satisfying equation). Also new R = rem(S') (same for all? Let's verify: transition_cost = abs(s[pos]-x) for both boundary and inside? For boundary, cost of first char is abs(s[pos]-x). For inside, cost = abs(s[pos]-c) where c=x. So transition_cost depends only on x and pos, not on state. Therefore R' = R - abs(s[pos]-x). Same for all transitions output x. Good. So we can maintain R.

- Inside states (c,r) for c 0..25, r 1..5 maybe. But not all possible; only those reachable by greedy prefix and optimal. Set size at most 1 + 26*5 = 131. Actually r can be 1..4? Need compute `rem_inside(pos, c, r)` quickly. We can precompute `blockCost(pos, r, c)` for r up to 5 using `cost_pref[c]`:
`blockCost = pref[c][pos+r] - pref[c][pos]`.
Then `rem = blockCost + g[pos+r]` if pos+r <= n else INF.

Need compute `g[pos]` suffix DP. We can compute using blockCost too:
```
g = [INF]*(n+1)
g[n]=0
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos+L
        if end <= n:
            base = g[end]
            # min over c of blockCost(pos,L,c) + base
            for c in range(26):
                cost = pref[c][end]-pref[c][pos] + base
                if cost < best: best = cost
    g[pos] = best
```
Since base independent of c. This is O(26*n*3). With `pref[c]` list, each blockCost O(1). 3.9M iterations, okay. But Python nested loops with list indexing maybe okay (<1s? maybe 2-3s). Need optimize.

Memory for pref: list of 26 lists of length n+1. We can build:
```
pref = [[0]*(n+1) for _ in range(26)]
for i,ch in enumerate(caption):
    val = ord(ch)-97
    for c in range(26):
        pref[c][i+1] = pref[c][i] + abs(val-c)
```

Suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1,-1,-1):
    best=INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end=pos+L
        if end<=n:
            base=g[end]
            for c in range(26):
                cost=base
                for t in range(pos,end):
                    cost += abs(A[t]-c)
                if cost<best: best=cost
    g[pos]=best
```

Let's think about correctness of suffix DP with block lengths 3,4,5. We need prove any good caption can be partitioned into blocks length 3,4,5. For each maximal run length L>=3, can express L as sum of 3s,4s,5s. Is that always true? Let's prove:
- L=3,4,5 base.
- L=6=3+3, 7=3+4, 8=4+4, 9=3+3+3 or 4+5, 10=5+5.
- For L>=11, L-3>=8 and by induction? More simply, since 3,4,5 include consecutive 3,4,5, all integers >=3? Wait 6,7,8 yes; for any L>=9, L-3>=6 and if L-3 representable then L representable. Base 3-8. So yes all L>=3. Good.

Conversely, concatenating blocks of length 3/4/5 each uniform yields a good caption because every maximal run is union of adjacent blocks of same char, and each block length >=3, so run length >=3. Even if a block of length 3 is adjacent to same char, run longer; if not, run length at least 3. Good.

Thus DP exact.

Now, lexicographically smallest via greedy automaton. Need ensure greedy state set method yields lexicographically smallest optimal string. Standard: At each position, among all optimal full strings consistent with chosen prefix, choose smallest possible next character. We maintain set of automaton states that are possible after chosen prefix and can be completed optimally. If we choose smallest x with at least one feasible transition, then there exists an optimal full string with prefix+x. Any optimal full string with smaller next char would have been feasible, so none. Then update states to all feasible transitions with char x; this preserves exactly the set of states possible after prefix+x. Thus greedy produces lexicographically smallest. Good.

Need implement state set efficiently.

Pseudo greedy:

```
INF = 10**15
# compute g
...
if g[0] >= INF: return ""

# possible states at current pos
states = [(0,0)]  # boundary represented as (0,0), r=0
R = g[0]
ans = []

for pos in range(n):
    # compute next_possible[26][5]? r 0..4
    next_possible = [[False]*5 for _ in range(26)]  # r 0..4
    # For each state
    for c, r in states:
        if r == 0:
            # boundary
            # rem = g[pos] should equal R
            for L in (3,4,5):
                end = pos + L
                if end > n: continue
                # for x in 0..25
                # cost_first = abs(A[pos]-x)
                # rem_next = blockCost(pos+1, L-1, x) + g[end]
                # if cost_first + rem_next == R: next_possible[x][L-1] = True
                # But note L-1 is 2,3,4
                for x in range(26):
                    cost_first = abs(A[pos]-x)
                    # blockCost from pos+1 to end-1 length L-1
                    rem_next = (pref[x][end] - pref[x][pos+1]) + g[end]
                    if cost_first + rem_next == R:
                        next_possible[x][L-1] = True
        else:
            # inside
            # rem = blockCost(pos,r,c)+g[pos+r] should equal R
            # next char c
            x = c
            cost_first = abs(A[pos]-x)
            if r-1 == 0:
                rem_next = g[pos+1]
                if cost_first + rem_next == R:
                    next_possible[0][0] = True  # boundary
            else:
                rem_next = (pref[x][pos+r] - pref[x][pos+1]) + g[pos+r]
                if cost_first + rem_next == R:
                    next_possible[x][r-1] = True
    # choose smallest x with any r
    chosen = None
    for x in range(26):
        if any(next_possible[x][r] for r in range(5)):
            chosen = x
            break
    if chosen is None:
        return "" # should not happen
    ans.append(chr(97+chosen))
    # update states and R
    new_states = []
    for r in range(5):
        if next_possible[chosen][r]:
            if r == 0:
                new_states.append((0,0))
            else:
                new_states.append((chosen,r))
    states = new_states
    R = R - abs(A[pos]-chosen)
return ''.join(ans)
```

Need check boundary transition equation using `pref[x][end] - pref[x][pos+1]` for blockCost(pos+1, L-1, x). Since end=pos+L. The interval pos+1..end-1 length L-1. pref[x][end] - pref[x][pos+1] gives sum abs for indices pos+1..end-1. Good. `cost_first + rem_next = abs(A[pos]-x) + blockCost(pos+1,L-1,x)+g[end] = blockCost(pos,L,x)+g[end]`. This equals g[pos] if this block choice is optimal for suffix. Since R = g[pos] for boundary state. Good.

For inside state, `rem_next` for r-1>0 uses `pref[x][pos+r] - pref[x][pos+1]`? Let's verify:
Current state at pos with r remaining including pos. The block extends from pos to pos+r-1. After outputting pos, next state at pos+1 has r-1 remaining including pos+1, block extends pos+1..pos+r-1. `pref[x][pos+r] - pref[x][pos+1]` gives sum indices pos+1..pos+r-1. Good. `g[pos+r]` because after finishing block at pos+r, boundary. If r-1=0, pos+r = pos+1, g[pos+1]. Good.

Need ensure `R` equals rem of all states. Initially R=g[0]. When we update R = R - abs(A[pos]-chosen), is it always equal to rem of new states? For a feasible transition, cost_first + rem_next = old R, so rem_next = old R - cost_first = R_next. Yes. Good.

Potential issue: In boundary transition, we set `next_possible[x][L-1] = True` for L=3,4,5. Potential issue: At boundary pos, if suffix length is exactly 3,4,5, g[pos] finite. Transition with L = n-pos should be feasible. If suffix length >5, can start block length 3/4/5. Good.

Potential issue: What if at boundary pos, `g[pos]` is INF (impossible suffix length 1/2). Greedy should not be at boundary with impossible suffix if total optimal. But if n=1/2, g[0]=INF return "". If during greedy, could choose a block that ends at pos with remaining suffix length 1/2? The boundary transition equation would require g[end] finite; if end=n? okay. If after finishing block, suffix length <3, g[pos+r] INF, so such transition not feasible. Thus states won't lead to impossible.

Now, suffix DP `g[pos]` with block lengths 3,4,5. Need compute `g[pos]` for all pos. We can compute from right to left using pref. But note if suffix length is 1 or 2, g[pos]=INF. If suffix length 0, g[n]=0. For pos where n-pos >=3, g[pos] finite? Is it always possible to make any suffix of length >=3 good? Yes choose any block length 3/4/5 covering first part and recursively; since all lengths >=3 representable, finite. Good.

Let's think about lexicographic tie-breaking with greedy. At each position, we choose smallest x with any feasible transition. Suppose there is a feasible transition from boundary starting block length 5 with x='a', and another from inside state with x='b'. We choose 'a'. Good. If x='a' feasible only via boundary L=5, and x='a' also feasible via inside? We update states to all feasible transitions with x='a'. This maintains all possible optimal strings with prefix chosen. Good.

Need consider if `R` can become negative? No, cost_first <= R for feasible.

Now, can we simplify by computing `g` using block lengths 3,4,5 and then using greedy. Need prove DP with g suffix independent of previous char. Since blocks can be adjacent same char, no restriction. If we required adjacent blocks different, g would need last char. But we don't. Is it always valid to allow adjacent blocks same char? Yes, because final string remains good; the partition is artificial. In DP, if two adjacent blocks same char, the actual run is longer. There's no issue. If we allowed adjacent blocks same char, we might represent a final string in multiple ways. That's fine. Does this ever produce a final string that is not good? No, each block length >=3 uniform, so every run is at least one block length >=3. Good.

Now, let's consider if block lengths 3,4,5 are sufficient for optimal cost and lexicographically smallest. Suppose a final good caption has a run length L. We can partition into blocks 3/4/5. The cost is same because chars unchanged. Thus any good caption can be represented by our block grammar. Conversely, any block grammar output is good. So min cost over grammar equals min cost over good captions. Lexicographically smallest over grammar equals over good captions because every good caption has at least one grammar representation; but a good caption might have multiple representations. Greedy over grammar may choose a representation that corresponds to same string; lexicographic order is over strings, not representations. If a string has multiple representations, greedy may maintain multiple states and still output the string. Good.

Potential issue: Our DP `g[pos]` uses min cost for suffix from pos, independent of the fact that if previous block has same char, the suffix's first block could merge with previous block. But merging doesn't affect cost or goodness. The suffix itself must be good? Wait if suffix starts immediately after a previous block of same char, and the suffix's first block is length 3, then the merged run length is previous block length + 3 >=6, good. If suffix's first block length is 3, it's good on its own too. So requiring suffix good is sufficient. If suffix's first block length maybe 2? We don't allow. So independent.

Now, let's think about operation cost: We assumed cost to change original char to target char c is abs difference. Operation can change to adjacent only, but repeated operations allow any distance, min operations = abs. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any possibility to change a character multiple times in a way that final target differs and cost less? No, min is abs. Operation count per position independent. Good.

Now, let's think about constraints n=5e4. Suffix DP O(n*26*3) with pref O(1). Greedy O(n * states * transitions). Now, let's examine suffix DP `g` computation in detail. We need `pref` for blockCost. Build `pref`:
```
A = [ord(ch)-97 for ch in caption]
pref = [[0]*(n+1) for _ in range(26)]
for i, val in enumerate(A):
    for c in range(26):
        pref[c][i+1] = pref[c][i] + abs(val-c)
```
This is 1.3M operations, fine. But note `pref[c][i+1]` for each c uses `pref[c][i]`; if we loop c inner, okay. Could build more efficiently by for each c compute prefix over A:
```
for c in range(26):
    pc = pref[c]
    s=0
    for i,val in enumerate(A):
        s += abs(val-c)
        pc[i+1]=s
```
This is also 1.3M. Which is faster? Outer c, inner n. Use local variables. We'll do:
```
pref = [[0]*(n+1) for _ in range(26)]
for c in range(26):
    pc = pref[c]
    s = 0
    for i, val in enumerate(A):
        s += abs(val - c)
        pc[i+1] = s
```
Good.

Suffix DP:
```
g = [INF]*(n+1)
g[n] = 0
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    # if n-pos < 3, remains INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            # min over c
            # local pref
            for c in range(26):
                cost = pref[c][end] - pref[c][pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g[pos] = best
```
Need handle if `base` INF; adding finite okay but INF+something > INF? We set INF large 10**15. If base INF, cost > INF, won't beat. But if best INF, cost INF+ maybe > INF, not < best (if best=INF, INF+ > INF, false). So no need skip. But to avoid overflow no issue. Use INF=10**15; max cost n*25=1.25e6, so INF safe.

Can we optimize min over c using precomputed `best_block[pos][L]`? Not necessary.

But wait: `g[pos]` for suffix length 3/4/5 can be computed as min over c of blockCost + g[end]. For pos near end, base=0. Good.

Now, greedy uses `g[pos]` and `pref`. Need ensure `pref[x][end] - pref[x][pos+1]` when pos+1 > end? For L=3, pos+1 < end. For inside r=1, we handle separately. For boundary L=3, pos+1 <= end-1? end=pos+3, pos+1=pos+1, interval length 2, okay. For L=3, L-1=2. For L=4 length3, L=5 length4. Good.

Now, let's think about possible state r=0 boundary representation in `next_possible`. We use `next_possible = [[False]*5 for _ in range(26)]`; r=0..4. For boundary next, set `next_possible[0][0] = True`. But if chosen x not 0 and r=0, we still need boundary state after choosing x. The boundary state doesn't depend on x. Now, when iterating states at current pos, state `(c,r)`:
- If r==0 boundary, c=0.
- If r>0, c is block char. At current pos, the next output char must be c. So in next_possible, we will set for x=c. Good.

Let's rewrite greedy with this representation.

Pseudo:
```
states = [(0,0)]  # boundary
R = g[0]
ans = []
for pos in range(n):
    next_possible = [[False]*5 for _ in range(26)]  # r 0..4
    for c,r in states:
        if r == 0:
            # boundary
            for L in (3,4,5):
                end = pos + L
                if end > n: continue
                # for each x
                # local variables for speed
                for x in range(26):
                    # cost_first = abs(A[pos]-x)
                    # rem_next = pref[x][end] - pref[x][pos+1] + g[end]
                    if abs(A[pos]-x) + (pref[x][end] - pref[x][pos+1]) + g[end] == R:
                        next_possible[x][L-1] = True
        else:
            x = c
            cost_first = abs(A[pos]-x)
            if r == 1:
                rem_next = g[pos+1]
            else:
                rem_next = pref[x][pos+r] - pref[x][pos+1] + g[pos+r]
            if cost_first + rem_next == R:
                if r == 1:
                    next_possible[x][0] = True
                else:
                    next_possible[x][r-1] = True
    chosen = -1
    for x in range(26):
        if any(next_possible[x]):
            chosen = x
            break
    if chosen == -1: return ""
    ans.append(chr(97+chosen))
    new_states = []
    if next_possible[chosen][0]:
        new_states.append((0,0))
    for r in range(1,5):
        if next_possible[chosen][r]:
            new_states.append((chosen,r))
    states = new_states
    R = R - abs(A[pos]-chosen)
return ''.join(ans)
```

Need verify for inside state with r>1, `rem_next = pref[x][pos+r] - pref[x][pos+1] + g[pos+r]`. But if r=2, pos+r = pos+2, interval pos+1..pos+1 length1. Good. If r=3, interval pos+1..pos+2 length2. If r=4, length3. If r=5 (not used), length4. Good.

But wait: For inside state at pos with r remaining, `rem_current = pref[x][pos+r] - pref[x][pos] + g[pos+r]`. We don't explicitly compute it; we rely on R. But if state was added, R should equal rem_current. However, due to multiple states with different rem? We argued all states have same R. Need ensure when we update states, we only keep states whose rem_next equals R_next. Since we set R_next = R - cost_first. For a transition satisfying cost_first + rem_next == R, rem_next = R_next. So all new states have rem R_next. Good. But in the next iteration, we don't recompute rem_current; we just use R. For inside state, is it guaranteed that `pref[x][pos+r] - pref[x][pos] + g[pos+r] == R`? It should by construction. But due to integer exact, yes. We don't need check. But if a state was added from boundary transition, rem_next was computed as `pref[x][end]-pref[x][pos+1]+g[end]` with r=L-1. At next pos (pos+1), rem_current for state (x,r) = pref[x][pos+1+r] - pref[x][pos+1] + g[pos+1+r]. Since pos+1+r = end, this equals rem_next. Good. If state was added from inside transition, similarly. So invariant holds.

Potential issue: At boundary transition, we check equality with `R` but R is g[pos] for boundary state. If states also include inside states, R same. Good.

Wait for L=3: after pos0 r=2, pos1 r=1, pos2 r=0 boundary at pos3. For L=4: after pos0 r=3, pos1 r=2, pos2 r=1, pos3 r=0? Let's recalc: Starting L=4 at pos0, after output pos0, state at pos1 r=3. Now, let's think about lexicographically smallest among min operations. Our greedy chooses smallest char at each position among all optimal completions. But is it possible that choosing a smaller char at current position forces a larger char later, while choosing a slightly larger char now yields much smaller later, and lexicographically the latter is smaller? No, lexicographic order prioritizes first differing position; smaller at current position always makes whole string smaller regardless of later. So greedy is correct.

Potential issue: In suffix DP, we compute min cost for suffix independent of prefix. But when greedy chooses a block, the suffix after the block is optimized independently. However, the suffix DP `g[pos]` computes min cost to make suffix from pos good on its own. However, in greedy, we use `pref[x][end] - pref[x][pos+1]` many times. `pref` is list of lists. Access `pref[x]` inside loop. We can optimize by local variables:
```
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Inside boundary loop:
```
for x in range(26):
    px = pref_local[x]
    if abs(A_local[pos] - x) + px[end] - px[pos+1] + g_local[end] == R:
        next_possible[x][L-1] = True
```
Note `px[end] - px[pos+1]` can be negative? No, prefix nondecreasing? abs values nonnegative, so yes.

Inside state:
```
px = pref_local[x]
if r == 1:
    rem_next = g_local[pos+1]
else:
    rem_next = px[pos+r] - px[pos+1] + g_local[pos+r]
```
Need if r==1, pos+r = pos+1, `px[pos+1]-px[pos+1]=0`, so could use same formula `px[pos+r]-px[pos+1] + g[pos+r]` = 0+g[pos+1]. So no special case needed! For r=1, `px[pos+1]-px[pos+1]=0`. Thus:
```
rem_next = pref[x][pos+r] - pref[x][pos+1] + g[pos+r]
```
works for r>=1. If r=1, pos+r=pos+1. Good. So inside transition:
```
x=c

rem_next = pref[x][pos+r] - pref[x][pos+1] + g[pos+r]
if cost_first + rem_next == R:
    next_possible[x][r-1] = True
```
For r=1, r-1=0, boundary. For r>1, inside. Great. But wait if r=1, `pref[x][pos+r] - pref[x][pos+1]` = pref[x][pos+1]-pref[x][pos+1]=0. Good.

Similarly, boundary transition with L: `rem_next = pref[x][end] - pref[x][pos+1] + g[end]`. This is same formula with r=L-1. So boundary transition is like starting a block and moving to inside state with r=L-1. Good.

Now, for boundary state, we loop L=3,4,5. But what if suffix length at pos is exactly 3,4,5 and there are multiple L? Now, let's think about if `g[pos]` can be achieved by starting a block of length L but with a char x, and then suffix from pos+L has min cost g[pos+L]. Our DP min over c and L. Greedy boundary transition checks exactly `abs(A[pos]-x) + blockCost(pos+1,L-1,x) + g[pos+L] == R`. This is equivalent to `blockCost(pos,L,x)+g[pos+L] == R`. Since R = g[pos]. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possibility of starting a block of length L but the suffix after the block is not independently optimal in the lexicographically smallest full string, even though total cost optimal? If suffix cost > g[pos+L], total cost would exceed g[pos] (since block cost fixed) and not optimal. If suffix cost = g[pos+L] but suffix string not lexicographically smallest among optimal suffixes, greedy will later choose lexicographically smallest suffix because it maintains all optimal states. So using g for feasibility is enough.

Now, let's think about if `g[pos]` computed as min over c of blockCost + g[pos+L] might choose a block char c and suffix that together produce a final string where adjacent blocks same char, but the partition has adjacent same char. That's allowed. No issue.

Now, let's consider if the lexicographically smallest optimal string might require a non-optimal suffix after a block to allow a smaller char at the boundary? No, lexicographic order after the block only matters after the block chars are fixed. For a fixed block, choosing a lexicographically smaller suffix (among min cost for that suffix) yields lexicographically smaller full string. If a suffix has higher cost, total cost higher, not allowed. So greedy with g works.

Now, let's rewrite greedy with encoded states.

```
states = [0]
R = g[0]
ans = []
for pos in range(n):
    next_possible = [False]*130
    for state in states:
        c = state // 5
        r = state % 5
        if r == 0:
            # boundary
            for L in (3,4,5):
                end = pos + L
                if end > n: continue
                # for x in 0..25
                for x in range(26):
                    px = pref[x]
                    # cost_first + rem_next == R
                    if abs(A[pos]-x) + px[end] - px[pos+1] + g[end] == R:
                        next_possible[x*5 + (L-1)] = True
        else:
            x = c
            px = pref[x]
            # rem_next
            # pos+r <= n invariant
            if abs(A[pos]-x) + px[pos+r] - px[pos+1] + g[pos+r] == R:
                next_possible[x*5 + (r-1)] = True
    chosen = -1
    for x in range(26):
        idx = x*5
        if next_possible[idx] or next_possible[idx+1] or next_possible[idx+2] or next_possible[idx+3] or next_possible[idx+4]:
            chosen = x
            break
    if chosen == -1:
        return ""
    ans.append(chr(97+chosen))
    new_states = []
    idx = chosen*5
    if next_possible[idx]:
        new_states.append(0)
    for r in range(1,5):
        if next_possible[idx+r]:
            new_states.append(chosen*5 + r)
    states = new_states
    R = R - abs(A[pos]-chosen)
return ''.join(ans)
```

Let's prove invariant more formally:
- Initially pos=0, states=[0], R=g[0].
- Suppose at pos, for every state S in states, rem(S)=R. For boundary, rem=g[pos]; for inside, rem=blockCost(pos,r,c)+g[pos+r].
- For a transition output x from S to S' with cost_first=abs(A[pos]-x), we add S' if cost_first + rem(S') == R. Then rem(S') = R - cost_first = R_next. Thus new states have rem=R_next. So invariant holds. Good.
Thus R equals rem for all states. We don't need compute rem_current.

Let's write final code carefully:

```
class Solution:
    def minCostGoodCaption(self, caption: str) -> str:
        n = len(caption)
        if n < 3:
            return ""
        A = [ord(ch) - 97 for ch in caption]
        INF = 10**15

        pref = [[0] * (n + 1) for _ in range(26)]
        for c in range(26):
            pc = pref[c]
            s = 0
            for i, val in enumerate(A):
                s += abs(val - c)
                pc[i + 1] = s

        g = [INF] * (n + 1)
        g[n] = 0
        for pos in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
            best = INF
            for L in (3, 4, 5):
                end = pos + L
                if end <= n:
                    base = g[end]
                    if base >= INF:
                        continue
                    for c in range(26):
                        pc = pref[c]
                        cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                        if cost < best:
                            best = cost
            g[pos] = best

        if g[0] >= INF:
            return ""

        states = [0]
        R = g[0]
        ans = []

        for pos in range(n):
            next_possible = [False] * 130
            for state in states:
                c = state // 5
                r = state % 5
                if r == 0:
                    for L in (3, 4, 5):
                        end = pos + L
                        if end > n:
                            continue
                        # g_end = g[end]
                        for x in range(26):
                            px = pref[x]
                            if abs(A[pos] - x) + px[end] - px[pos + 1] + g[end] == R:
                                next_possible[x * 5 + (L - 1)] = True
                else:
                    x = c
                    px = pref[x]
                    if abs(A[pos] - x) + px[pos + r] - px[pos + 1] + g[pos + r] == R:
                        next_possible[x * 5 + (r - 1)] = True

            chosen = -1
            for x in range(26):
                idx = x * 5
                if next_possible[idx] or next_possible[idx + 1] or next_possible[idx + 2] or next_possible[idx + 3] or next_possible[idx + 4]:
                    chosen = x
                    break
            if chosen == -1:
                return ""

            ans.append(chr(97 + chosen))
            idx = chosen * 5
            new_states = []
            if next_possible[idx]:
                new_states.append(0)
            for r in range(1, 5):
                if next_possible[idx + r]:
                    new_states.append(chosen * 5 + r)
            states = new_states
            R = R - abs(A[pos] - chosen)

        return ''.join(ans)
```

Need check inside transition `px[pos + r] - px[pos + 1]` when r=1, pos+r=pos+1; okay. If r=0 not inside. Good.

...

```

g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...

...
```
Good.

```
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...
if abs(A_local[pos] - x) + px[end] - px[pos+1] + g_local[end] == R:
...
```
Good.

Now, consider a case where boundary and inside states have different possible next chars. Example n=7, original maybe forces if boundary at pos3 choose 'z', but inside from L=4 outputs 'a'. Greedy at pos3 chooses 'a' if feasible. Let's construct original such that both L=3 and L=4 at pos0 are optimal but with different chars? Actually if pos0 starts 'a' with L=4, at pos3 inside outputs 'a'. If pos0 starts 'a' with L=3, at pos3 boundary may choose 'z'. Both total cost g0. Greedy at pos3 chooses 'a', so final string has first four chars 'a', i.e., L=4. This is lexicographically smaller than L=3 with 'z' at pos3. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "g" suffix DP if `pref` is list of lists and `pref[c]` inside c loop; if we localize `pref_local = pref`, `g_local = g`, `A_local = A` maybe. We'll include local variables for speed:
```
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...
if abs(A_local[pos] - x) + px[end] - px[pos+1] + g_local[end] == R:
...
```
Good.

```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...

...
```

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "g" suffix DP if `pref` is list of lists and `pref[c]` inside c loop; if we localize `pref_local = pref`, `g_local = g`, `A_local = A` maybe. We'll include local variables for speed:
```
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...
if abs(A_local[pos] - x) + px[end] - px[pos+1] + g_local[end] == R:
...
```
Good.

```

g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...

...
```
Good.

```
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...
if abs(A_local[pos] - x) + px[end] - px[pos+1] + g_local[end] == R:
...
```
Good.

```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```

```

pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...

```

g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...

...
```
Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "g" suffix DP if `pref` is list of lists and `pref[c]` inside c loop; if we localize `pref_local = pref`, `g_local = g`, `A_local = A` maybe. We'll include local variables for speed:
```
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...
if abs(A_local[pos] - x) + px[end] - px[pos+1] + g_local[end] == R:
...
```
Good.

```
class Solution:
    def minCostGoodCaption(self, caption: str) -> str:
        n = len(caption)
        if n < 3:
            return ""

        A = [ord(ch) - 97 for ch in caption]
        INF = 10**15

        pref = [[0] * (n + 1) for _ in range(26)]
        for c in range(26):
            pc = pref[c]
            s = 0
            for i, val in enumerate(A):
                s += abs(val - c)
                pc[i + 1] = s

        g = [INF] * (n + 1)
        g[n] = 0
        for pos in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
            best = INF
            for L in (3, 4, 5):
                end = pos + L
                if end <= n:
                    base = g[end]
                    if base >= INF:
                        continue
                    for c in range(26):
                        pc = pref[c]
                        cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                        if cost < best:
                            best = cost
            g[pos] = best

        if g[0] >= INF:
            return ""

        states = [0]
        R = g[0]
        ans = []

        for pos in range(n):
            next_possible = [False] * 130

            for state in states:
                c = state // 5
                r = state % 5

                if r == 0:
                    for L in (3, 4, 5):
                        end = pos + L
                        if end > n:
                            continue
                        g_end = g[end]
                        for x in range(26):
                            px = pref[x]
                            if abs(A[pos] - x) + px[end] - px[pos + 1] + g_end == R:
                                next_possible[x * 5 + (L - 1)] = True
                else:
                    x = c
                    px = pref[x]
                    if abs(A[pos] - x) + px[pos + r] - px[pos + 1] + g[pos + r] == R:
                        next_possible[x * 5 + (r - 1)] = True

            chosen = -1
            for x in range(26):
                idx = x * 5
                if next_possible[idx] or next_possible[idx + 1] or next_possible[idx + 2] or next_possible[idx + 3] or next_possible[idx + 4]:
                    chosen = x
                    break

            if chosen == -1:
                return ""

            ans.append(chr(97 + chosen))

            idx = chosen * 5
            new_states = []
            if next_possible[idx]:
                new_states.append(0)
            for r in range(1, 5):
                if next_possible[idx + r]:
                    new_states.append(chosen * 5 + r)

            states = new_states
            R = R - abs(A[pos] - chosen)

        return ''.join(ans)
```

pos3 states boundary, inside r=1. Boundary R=0. Boundary transitions: suffix length4, L=4 feasible (L=3 end6? suffix len1 INF; L=5 end8>n). So boundary can start L=4 x='a' -> next r=3. Inside r=1 outputs a -> boundary. next_possible a r=3 and boundary. states after pos3: boundary, inside r=3. R=0.
pos4 states boundary, inside r=3. Boundary suffix length3, L=3 feasible -> next r=2. Inside r=3 outputs a -> next r=2. next_possible a r=2 (both). states after pos4: inside r=2. R=0.
pos5 inside r=2 -> next r=1. pos6 inside r=1 -> boundary. Output all a. Good. State set sometimes includes boundary and inside with same R=0. Works.

Now, consider a case where boundary and inside states have different possible next chars. Example n=7, original maybe forces if boundary at pos3 choose 'z', but inside from L=4 outputs 'a'. Greedy at pos3 chooses 'a' if feasible. Let's construct original such that both L=3 and L=4 at pos0 are optimal but with different chars? Actually if pos0 starts 'a' with L=4, at pos3 inside outputs 'a'. If pos0 starts 'a' with L=3, at pos3 boundary may choose 'z'. Both total cost g0. Greedy at pos3 chooses 'a', so final string has first four chars 'a', i.e., L=4. This is lexicographically smaller than L=3 with 'z' at pos3. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "g" suffix DP if `pref` is list of lists and `pref[c]` inside c loop; if we localize `pref_local = pref`, `g_local = g`, `A_local = A` maybe. We'll include local variables for speed:
```
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...
if abs(A_local[pos] - x) + px[end] - px[pos+1] + g_local[end] == R:
...
```
Good.

```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...
if abs(A_local[pos] - x) + px[end] - px[pos+1] + g_local[end] == R:
...
```
Good.

            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```

g_local = g
...

...
```

```

g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...

...
```
Good.

```
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...
if abs(A_local[pos] - x) + px[end] - px[pos+1] + g_local[end] == R:
...
```
Good.

```

g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...

...
```
Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a known official solution that uses DP with `dp[i][j]` and reconstructs by comparing strings, but our solution is more complex. Is there any chance our greedy automaton with suffix DP fails because the grammar is ambiguous and the state set after choosing prefix may include states with different "remaining cost" but same total due to prefix cost? We argued same. Let's test with a concrete ambiguous case: n=7, all 'a'. g0=0. Greedy:
pos0 states [0], R=0. Boundary transitions L=3,4 feasible (L=5? end5 g5 suffix len2 INF, so not feasible for n=7? Wait n=7, L=5 end5 suffix length2 INF, so not feasible. So only L=3,4). next_possible for x='a': r=2 and r=3. states after pos0: state a r=2 (state 0*5+2=2) and a r=3 (state3). R=0.
pos1 states 2,3. Both output a. For state r=2: rem_next = pref[a][3]-pref[a][2]+g3 =0; next r=1. For state r=3: rem_next = pref[a][4]-pref[a][2]+g4 =0; next r=2. next_possible a r=1 and r=2. states after pos1: r=1,2. R=0.
pos2 states r=1,2. For r=1: rem_next = g3=0; next boundary. For r=2: rem_next = pref[a][4]-pref[a][3]+g4=0; next r=1. next_possible a boundary and r=1. states after pos2: boundary 0, inside r=1. R=0.
pos3 states boundary, inside r=1. Boundary R=0. Boundary transitions: suffix length4, L=4 feasible (L=3 end6? suffix len1 INF; L=5 end8>n). So boundary can start L=4 x='a' -> next r=3. Inside r=1 outputs a -> boundary. next_possible a r=3 and boundary. states after pos3: boundary, inside r=3. R=0.
pos4 states boundary, inside r=3. Boundary suffix length3, L=3 feasible -> next r=2. Inside r=3 outputs a -> next r=2. next_possible a r=2 (both). states after pos4: inside r=2. R=0.
pos5 inside r=2 -> next r=1. pos6 inside r=1 -> boundary. Output all a. Good. State set sometimes includes boundary and inside with same R=0. Works.

Now, consider a case where boundary and inside states have different possible next chars. Example n=7, original maybe forces if boundary at pos3 choose 'z', but inside from L=4 outputs 'a'. Greedy at pos3 chooses 'a' if feasible. Let's construct original such that both L=3 and L=4 at pos0 are optimal but with different chars? Actually if pos0 starts 'a' with L=4, at pos3 inside outputs 'a'. If pos0 starts 'a' with L=3, at pos3 boundary may choose 'z'. Both total cost g0. Greedy at pos3 chooses 'a', so final string has first four chars 'a', i.e., L=4. This is lexicographically smaller than L=3 with 'z' at pos3. Good.

Can such a situation occur with same cost? Suppose original positions 0..6 are such that changing 0..2 to 'a' and 3..6 to 'z' costs X, and changing 0..3 to 'a' and 4..6 to something costs X. Yes possible. Greedy handles.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "g" suffix DP if `pref` is list of lists and `pref[c]` inside c loop; if we localize `pref_local = pref`, `g_local = g`, `A_local = A` maybe. We'll include local variables for speed:
```
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
A_local = A
```
Then use. In suffix DP:
```
for pos in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    best = INF
    for L in (3,4,5):
        end = pos + L
        if end <= n:
            base = g_local[end]
            if base >= INF: continue
            for c in range(26):
                pc = pref_local[c]
                cost = pc[end] - pc[pos] + base
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
    g_local[pos] = best
```
In greedy:
```
A_local = A
pref_local = pref
g_local = g
...
if abs(A_local[pos] - x) + px[end] - px[pos+1] + g_local[end] == R:
...
```
Good.
